Go to Header
type <1>Header</1> <2>¶</2>
A Header represents the key-value pairs in an HTTP header.
The keys should be in canonical form, as returned by <1>CanonicalHeaderKey</1>.
Go to Header.Add
func (Header) <1>Add</1> <2>¶</2>
Add adds the key, value pair to the header. It appends to any existing values associated with key. The key is case insensitive; it is canonicalized by <1>CanonicalHeaderKey</1>.
Go to Header.Clone
func (Header) <1>Clone</1> <2>¶</2>
<1>added in</1> <2>go1.13</2>
Clone returns a copy of h or nil if h is nil.
Go to Header.Del
func (Header) <1>Del</1> <2>¶</2>
Del deletes the values associated with key. The key is case insensitive; it is canonicalized by <1>CanonicalHeaderKey</1>.
Go to Header.Get
func (Header) <1>Get</1> <2>¶</2>
Get gets the first value associated with the given key. If there are no values associated with the key, Get returns "". It is case insensitive; <1>textproto.CanonicalMIMEHeaderKey</1> is used to canonicalize the provided key. Get assumes that all keys are stored in canonical form. To use non-canonical keys, access the map directly.
Go to Header.Set
func (Header) <1>Set</1> <2>¶</2>
Set sets the header entries associated with key to the single element value. It replaces any existing values associated with key. The key is case insensitive; it is canonicalized by <1>textproto.CanonicalMIMEHeaderKey</1>. To use non-canonical keys, assign to the map directly.
Go to Header.Values
func (Header) <1>Values</1> <2>¶</2>
<1>added in</1> <2>go1.14</2>
Values returns all values associated with the given key. It is case insensitive; <1>textproto.CanonicalMIMEHeaderKey</1> is used to canonicalize the provided key. To use non-canonical keys, access the map directly. The returned slice is not a copy.
Go to Header.Write
func (Header) <1>Write</1> <2>¶</2>
Write writes a header in wire format.
Go to Header.WriteSubset
func (Header) <1>WriteSubset</1> <2>¶</2>
WriteSubset writes a header in wire format. If exclude is not nil, keys where exclude[key] == true are not written. Keys are not canonicalized before checking the exclude map.
Go to Hijacker
type <1>Hijacker</1> <2>¶</2>
The Hijacker interface is implemented by ResponseWriters that allow an HTTP handler to take over the connection.
The default <1>ResponseWriter</1> for HTTP/1.x connections supports Hijacker, but HTTP/2 connections intentionally do not. ResponseWriter wrappers may also not support Hijacker. Handlers should always test for this ability at runtime.

Go to Protocols
type <1>Protocols</1> <2>¶</2>
Protocols is a set of HTTP protocols. The zero value is an empty set of protocols.
The supported protocols are:
HTTP1 is the HTTP/1.0 and HTTP/1.1 protocols. HTTP1 is supported on both unsecured TCP and secured TLS connections.
HTTP2 is the HTTP/2 protocol over a TLS connection.
UnencryptedHTTP2 is the HTTP/2 protocol over an unsecured TCP connection.

type <1>Pusher</1> <2>¶</2>

Go to Request
type <1>Request</1> <2>¶</2>
A Request represents an HTTP request received by a server or to be sent by a client.
The field semantics differ slightly between client and server usage. In addition to the notes on the fields below, see the documentation for <1>Request.Write</1> and <2>RoundTripper</2>.
Go to NewRequest
func <1>NewRequest</1> <2>¶</2>
NewRequest wraps <1>NewRequestWithContext</1> using <2>context.Background</2>.
Go to NewRequestWithContext
func <1>NewRequestWithContext</1> <2>¶</2>
NewRequestWithContext returns a new <1>Request</1> given a method, URL, and optional body.
If the provided body is also an <1>io.Closer</1>, the returned <2>Request.Body</2> is set to body and will be closed (possibly asynchronously) by the Client methods Do, Post, and PostForm, and <3>Transport.RoundTrip</3>.
NewRequestWithContext returns a Request suitable for use with <1>Client.Do</1> or <2>Transport.RoundTrip</2>. To create a request for use with testing a Server Handler, either use the <3>net/http/httptest.NewRequest</3> function, use <4>ReadRequest</4>, or manually update the Request fields. For an outgoing client request, the context controls the entire lifetime of a request and its response: obtaining a connection, sending the request, and reading the response headers and body. See the <5>Request</5> type's documentation for the difference between inbound and outbound request fields.
If body is of type <1>*bytes.Buffer</1>, <2>*bytes.Reader</2>, or <3>*strings.Reader</3>, the returned request's ContentLength is set to its exact value (instead of -1), GetBody is populated (so 307 and 308 redirects can replay the body), and Body is set to <4>NoBody</4> if the ContentLength is 0.
Go to ReadRequest
func <1>ReadRequest</1> <2>¶</2>
ReadRequest reads and parses an incoming request from b.
ReadRequest is a low-level function and should only be used for specialized applications; most code should use the <1>Server</1> to read requests and handle them via the <2>Handler</2> interface. ReadRequest only supports HTTP/1.x requests. For HTTP/2, use golang.org/x/net/http2.
Go to Request.AddCookie
func (*Request) <1>AddCookie</1> <2>¶</2>
AddCookie adds a cookie to the request. Per <1>RFC 6265 section 5.4</1>, AddCookie does not attach more than one <2>Cookie</2> header field. That means all cookies, if any, are written into the same line, separated by semicolon. AddCookie only sanitizes c's name and value, and does not sanitize a Cookie header already present in the request.
Go to Request.BasicAuth
func (*Request) <1>BasicAuth</1> <2>¶</2>
<1>added in</1> <2>go1.4</2>
BasicAuth returns the username and password provided in the request's Authorization header, if the request uses HTTP Basic Authentication. See <1>RFC 2617, Section 2</1>.
Go to Request.Clone
func (*Request) <1>Clone</1> <2>¶</2>
Clone returns a deep copy of r with its context changed to ctx. The provided ctx must be non-nil.
Clone only makes a shallow copy of the Body field.
For an outgoing client request, the context controls the entire lifetime of a request and its response: obtaining a connection, sending the request, and reading the response headers and body.
Go to Request.Context
func (*Request) <1>Context</1> <2>¶</2>
<1>added in</1> <2>go1.7</2>
Context returns the request's context. To change the context, use <1>Request.Clone</1> or <2>Request.WithContext</2>.
The returned context is always non-nil; it defaults to the background context.
For outgoing client requests, the context controls cancellation.
For incoming server requests, the context is canceled when the client's connection closes, the request is canceled (with HTTP/2), or when the ServeHTTP method returns.
Go to Request.Cookie
func (*Request) <1>Cookie</1> <2>¶</2>
Cookie returns the named cookie provided in the request or <1>ErrNoCookie</1> if not found. If multiple cookies match the given name, only one cookie will be returned.
Go to Request.Cookies
func (*Request) <1>Cookies</1> <2>¶</2>
Cookies parses and returns the HTTP cookies sent with the request.
Go to Request.CookiesNamed
func (*Request) <1>CookiesNamed</1> <2>¶</2>
CookiesNamed parses and returns the named HTTP cookies sent with the request or an empty slice if none matched.
Go to Request.FormFile
func (*Request) <1>FormFile</1> <2>¶</2>
FormFile returns the first file for the provided form key. FormFile calls <1>Request.ParseMultipartForm</1> and <2>Request.ParseForm</2> if necessary.
Go to Request.FormValue
func (*Request) <1>FormValue</1> <2>¶</2>
FormValue returns the first value for the named component of the query. The precedence order:
application/x-www-form-urlencoded form body (POST, PUT, PATCH only)
query parameters (always)
multipart/form-data form body (always)
FormValue calls <1>Request.ParseMultipartForm</1> and <2>Request.ParseForm</2> if necessary and ignores any errors returned by these functions. If key is not present, FormValue returns the empty string. To access multiple values of the same key, call ParseForm and then inspect <3>Request.Form</3> directly.
Go to Request.MultipartReader
func (*Request) <1>MultipartReader</1> <2>¶</2>
MultipartReader returns a MIME multipart reader if this is a multipart/form-data or a multipart/mixed POST request, else returns nil and an error. Use this function instead of <1>Request.ParseMultipartForm</1> to process the request body as a stream.
Go to Request.ParseForm
func (*Request) <1>ParseForm</1> <2>¶</2>
ParseForm populates r.Form and r.PostForm.
For all requests, ParseForm parses the raw query from the URL and updates r.Form.
For POST, PUT, and PATCH requests, it also reads the request body, parses it as a form and puts the results into both r.PostForm and r.Form. Request body parameters take precedence over URL query string values in r.Form.
If the request Body's size has not already been limited by <1>MaxBytesReader</1>, the size is capped at 10MB.
For other HTTP methods, or when the Content-Type is not application/x-www-form-urlencoded, the request Body is not read, and r.PostForm is initialized to a non-nil, empty value.
<1>Request.ParseMultipartForm</1> calls ParseForm automatically. ParseForm is idempotent.
Go to Request.ParseMultipartForm
func (*Request) <1>ParseMultipartForm</1> <2>¶</2>
ParseMultipartForm parses a request body as multipart/form-data. The whole request body is parsed and up to a total of maxMemory bytes of its file parts are stored in memory, with the remainder stored on disk in temporary files. ParseMultipartForm calls <1>Request.ParseForm</1> if necessary. If ParseForm returns an error, ParseMultipartForm returns it but also continues parsing the request body. After one call to ParseMultipartForm, subsequent calls have no effect.
Go to Request.PathValue
func (*Request) <1>PathValue</1> <2>¶</2>
PathValue returns the value for the named path wildcard in the <1>ServeMux</1> pattern that matched the request. It returns the empty string if the request was not matched against a pattern or there is no such wildcard in the pattern.
The value is unescaped. For example, if the pattern "/b/&#123;bucket&#125;" matches the path "/b/a%2fb", PathValue("bucket") returns "a/b".
Go to Request.PostFormValue
func (*Request) <1>PostFormValue</1> <2>¶</2>
PostFormValue returns the first value for the named component of the POST, PUT, or PATCH request body. URL query parameters are ignored. PostFormValue calls <1>Request.ParseMultipartForm</1> and <2>Request.ParseForm</2> if necessary and ignores any errors returned by these functions. If key is not present, PostFormValue returns the empty string.
Go to Request.ProtoAtLeast
func (*Request) <1>ProtoAtLeast</1> <2>¶</2>
ProtoAtLeast reports whether the HTTP protocol used in the request is at least major.minor.
Go to Request.Referer
func (*Request) <1>Referer</1> <2>¶</2>
Referer returns the referring URL, if sent in the request.
Referer is misspelled as in the request itself, a mistake from the earliest days of HTTP. This value can also be fetched from the <1>Header</1> map as Header["Referer"]; the benefit of making it available as a method is that the compiler can diagnose programs that use the alternate (correct English) spelling req.Referrer() but cannot diagnose programs that use Header["Referrer"].
Go to Request.SetBasicAuth
func (*Request) <1>SetBasicAuth</1> <2>¶</2>
SetBasicAuth sets the request's Authorization header to use HTTP Basic Authentication with the provided username and password.
With HTTP Basic Authentication the provided username and password are not encrypted. It should generally only be used in an HTTPS request.
The username may not contain a colon. Some protocols may impose additional requirements on pre-escaping the username and password. For instance, when used with OAuth2, both arguments must be URL encoded first with <1>url.QueryEscape</1>.
Go to Request.SetPathValue
func (*Request) <1>SetPathValue</1> <2>¶</2>
SetPathValue sets name to value, so that subsequent calls to r.PathValue(name) return value. It does not unescape value.
Go to Request.UserAgent

UserAgent returns the client's User-Agent, if sent in the request.
Go to Request.WithContext
func (*Request) <1>WithContext</1> <2>¶</2>
WithContext returns a shallow copy of r with its context changed to ctx. The provided ctx must be non-nil.
For outgoing client request, the context controls the entire lifetime of a request and its response: obtaining a connection, sending the request, and reading the response headers and body.

Go to Request.Write
func (*Request) <1>Write</1> <2>¶</2>
Write writes an HTTP/1.1 request, which is the header and body, in wire format. This method consults the following fields of the request:
If Body is present, Content-Length is &lt;= 0 and <1>Request.TransferEncoding</1> hasn't been set to "identity", Write adds "Transfer-Encoding: chunked" to the header. Body is closed after it is sent.
Header values for Host, Content-Length, Transfer-Encoding, and Trailer are not used; these are derived from other Request fields. If the Header does not contain a User-Agent value, Write uses "Go-http-client/1.1".
Go to Request.WriteProxy
func (*Request) <1>WriteProxy</1> <2>¶</2>
WriteProxy is like <1>Request.Write</1> but writes the request in the form expected by an HTTP proxy. In particular, <2>Request.WriteProxy</2> writes the initial Request-URI line of the request with an absolute URI, per section 5.3 of <3>RFC 7230</3>, including the scheme and host. In either case, WriteProxy also writes a Host header, using either r.Host or r.URL.Host.
Go to Response
type <1>Response</1> <2>¶</2>
Response represents the response from an HTTP request.
The <1>Client</1> and <2>Transport</2> return Responses from servers once the response headers have been received. The response body is streamed on demand as the Body field is read.
Go to Get
func <1>Get</1> <2>¶</2>
Get issues a GET to the specified URL. If the response is one of the following redirect codes, Get follows the redirect, up to a maximum of 10 redirects:
An error is returned if there were too many redirects or if there was an HTTP protocol error. A non-2xx response doesn't cause an error. Any returned error will be of type <1>*url.Error</1>. The url.Error value's Timeout method will report true if the request timed out.
Get is a wrapper around DefaultClient.Get.
To make a request with custom headers, use <1>NewRequest</1> and DefaultClient.Do.
To make a request with a specified context.Context, use <1>NewRequestWithContext</1> and DefaultClient.Do.

Go to ResponseController.EnableFullDuplex
func (*ResponseController) <1>EnableFullDuplex</1> <2>¶</2>
EnableFullDuplex indicates that the request handler will interleave reads from <1>Request.Body</1> with writes to the <2>ResponseWriter</2>.
For HTTP/1 requests, the Go HTTP server by default consumes any unread portion of the request body before beginning to write the response, preventing handlers from concurrently reading from the request and writing the response. Calling EnableFullDuplex disables this behavior and permits handlers to continue to read from the request while concurrently writing the response.
For HTTP/2 requests, the Go HTTP server always permits concurrent reads and responses.

SetReadDeadline sets the deadline for reading the entire request, including the body. Reads from the request body after the deadline has been exceeded will return an error. A zero value means no deadline.
Setting the read deadline after it has been exceeded will not extend it.
Go to ResponseController.SetWriteDeadline

Go to RoundTripper
type <1>RoundTripper</1> <2>¶</2>
RoundTripper is an interface representing the ability to execute a single HTTP transaction, obtaining the <1>Response</1> for a given <2>Request</2>.
A RoundTripper must be safe for concurrent use by multiple goroutines.
DefaultTransport is the default implementation of <1>Transport</1> and is used by <2>DefaultClient</2>. It establishes network connections as needed and caches them for reuse by subsequent calls. It uses HTTP proxies as directed by the environment variables HTTP_PROXY, HTTPS_PROXY and NO_PROXY (or the lowercase versions thereof).

Go to SameSite
type <1>SameSite</1> <2>¶</2>
<1>added in</1> <2>go1.11</2>
SameSite allows a server to define a cookie attribute making it impossible for the browser to send this cookie along with cross-site requests. The main goal is to mitigate the risk of cross-origin information leakage, and provide some protection against cross-site request forgery attacks.
See <1>https://tools.ietf.org/html/draft-ietf-httpbis-cookie-same-site-00</1> for details.
Go to ServeMux
type <1>ServeMux</1> <2>¶</2>
ServeMux is an HTTP request multiplexer. It matches the URL of each incoming request against a list of registered patterns and calls the handler for the pattern that most closely matches the URL.
Go to Patterns
Patterns <1>¶</1>
Patterns can match the method, host and path of a request. Some examples:
"/index.html" matches the path "/index.html" for any host and method.
"GET /static/" matches a GET request whose path begins with "/static/".
"example.com/" matches any request to the host "example.com".
"example.com/&#123;$&#125;" matches requests with host "example.com" and path "/".
"/b/&#123;bucket&#125;/o/&#123;objectname...&#125;" matches paths whose first segment is "b" and whose third segment is "o". The name "bucket" denotes the second segment and "objectname" denotes the remainder of the path.
In general, a pattern looks like
All three parts are optional; "/" is a valid pattern. If METHOD is present, it must be followed by at least one space or tab.
Literal (that is, non-wildcard) parts of a pattern match the corresponding parts of a request case-sensitively.
A pattern with no method matches every method. A pattern with the method GET matches both GET and HEAD requests. Otherwise, the method must match exactly.
A pattern with no host matches every host. A pattern with a host matches URLs on that host only.
A path can include wildcard segments of the form &#123;NAME&#125; or &#123;NAME...&#125;. For example, "/b/&#123;bucket&#125;/o/&#123;objectname...&#125;". The wildcard name must be a valid Go identifier. Wildcards must be full path segments: they must be preceded by a slash and followed by either a slash or the end of the string. For example, "/b_&#123;bucket&#125;" is not a valid pattern.
Normally a wildcard matches only a single path segment, ending at the next literal slash (not %2F) in the request URL. But if the "..." is present, then the wildcard matches the remainder of the URL path, including slashes. (Therefore it is invalid for a "..." wildcard to appear anywhere but at the end of a pattern.) The match for a wildcard can be obtained by calling <1>Request.PathValue</1> with the wildcard's name. A trailing slash in a path acts as an anonymous "..." wildcard.
The special wildcard &#123;$&#125; matches only the end of the URL. For example, the pattern "/&#123;$&#125;" matches only the path "/", whereas the pattern "/" matches every path.
For matching, both pattern paths and incoming request paths are unescaped segment by segment. So, for example, the path "/a%2Fb/100%25" is treated as having two segments, "a/b" and "100%". The pattern "/a%2fb/" matches it, but the pattern "/a/b/" does not.
Go to Precedence
Precedence <1>¶</1>
If two or more patterns match a request, then the most specific pattern takes precedence. A pattern P1 is more specific than P2 if P1 matches a strict subset of P2’s requests; that is, if P2 matches all the requests of P1 and more. If neither is more specific, then the patterns conflict. There is one exception to this rule, for backwards compatibility: if two patterns would otherwise conflict and one has a host while the other does not, then the pattern with the host takes precedence. If a pattern passed to <1>ServeMux.Handle</1> or <2>ServeMux.HandleFunc</2> conflicts with another pattern that is already registered, those functions panic.
As an example of the general rule, "/images/thumbnails/" is more specific than "/images/", so both can be registered. The former matches paths beginning with "/images/thumbnails/" and the latter will match any other path in the "/images/" subtree.
As another example, consider the patterns "GET /" and "/index.html": both match a GET request for "/index.html", but the former pattern matches all other GET and HEAD requests, while the latter matches any request for "/index.html" that uses a different method. The patterns conflict.
Go to Trailing-slash redirection
Trailing-slash redirection <1>¶</1>
Consider a <1>ServeMux</1> with a handler for a subtree, registered using a trailing slash or "..." wildcard. If the ServeMux receives a request for the subtree root without a trailing slash, it redirects the request by adding the trailing slash. This behavior can be overridden with a separate registration for the path without the trailing slash or "..." wildcard. For example, registering "/images/" causes ServeMux to redirect a request for "/images" to "/images/", unless "/images" has been registered separately.
Go to Request sanitizing
Request sanitizing <1>¶</1>
ServeMux also takes care of sanitizing the URL request path and the Host header, stripping the port number and redirecting any request containing . or .. segments or repeated slashes to an equivalent, cleaner URL. Escaped path elements such as "%2e" for "." and "%2f" for "/" are preserved and aren't considered separators for request routing.
Go to Compatibility
Compatibility <1>¶</1>
The pattern syntax and matching behavior of ServeMux changed significantly in Go 1.22. To restore the old behavior, set the GODEBUG environment variable to "httpmuxgo121=1". This setting is read once, at program startup; changes during execution will be ignored.
The backwards-incompatible changes include:
Wildcards are just ordinary literal path segments in 1.21. For example, the pattern "/&#123;x&#125;" will match only that path in 1.21, but will match any one-segment path in 1.22.
In 1.21, no pattern was rejected, unless it was empty or conflicted with an existing pattern. In 1.22, syntactically invalid patterns will cause <1>ServeMux.Handle</1> and <2>ServeMux.HandleFunc</2> to panic. For example, in 1.21, the patterns "/&#123;" and "/a&#123;x&#125;" match themselves, but in 1.22 they are invalid and will cause a panic when registered.
In 1.22, each segment of a pattern is unescaped; this was not done in 1.21. For example, in 1.22 the pattern "/%61" matches the path "/a" ("%61" being the URL escape sequence for "a"), but in 1.21 it would match only the path "/%2561" (where "%25" is the escape for the percent sign).
When matching patterns to paths, in 1.22 each segment of the path is unescaped; in 1.21, the entire path is unescaped. This change mostly affects how paths with %2F escapes adjacent to slashes are treated. See <1>https://go.dev/issue/21955</1> for details.
Go to NewServeMux
func <1>NewServeMux</1> <2>¶</2>
NewServeMux allocates and returns a new <1>ServeMux</1>.
Go to ServeMux.Handle
func (*ServeMux) <1>Handle</1> <2>¶</2>
Handle registers the handler for the given pattern. If the given pattern conflicts with one that is already registered or if the pattern is invalid, Handle panics.
See <1>ServeMux</1> for details on valid patterns and conflict rules.
Go to ServeMux.HandleFunc
func (*ServeMux) <1>HandleFunc</1> <2>¶</2>
HandleFunc registers the handler function for the given pattern. If the given pattern conflicts with one that is already registered or if the pattern is invalid, HandleFunc panics.
Go to ServeMux.Handler
func (*ServeMux) <1>Handler</1> <2>¶</2>
Handler returns the handler to use for the given request, consulting r.Method, r.Host, and r.URL.Path. It always returns a non-nil handler. If the path is not in its canonical form, the handler will be an internally-generated handler that redirects to the canonical path. If the host contains a port, it is ignored when matching handlers.
The path and host are used unchanged for CONNECT requests.
Handler also returns the registered pattern that matches the request or, in the case of internally-generated redirects, the path that will match after following the redirect.
If there is no registered handler that applies to the request, Handler returns a “page not found” or “method not supported” handler and an empty pattern.
Handler does not modify its argument. In particular, it does not populate named path wildcards, so r.PathValue will always return the empty string.
Go to ServeMux.ServeHTTP
func (*ServeMux) <1>ServeHTTP</1> <2>¶</2>
ServeHTTP dispatches the request to the handler whose pattern most closely matches the request URL.
Go to Server
type <1>Server</1> <2>¶</2>
A Server defines parameters for running an HTTP server. The zero value for Server is a valid configuration.
Go to Server.Close
func (*Server) <1>Close</1> <2>¶</2>
Close immediately closes all active net.Listeners and any connections in state <1>StateNew</1>, <2>StateActive</2>, or <3>StateIdle</3>. For a graceful shutdown, use <4>Server.Shutdown</4>.
Close does not attempt to close (and does not even know about) any hijacked connections, such as WebSockets.
Close returns any error returned from closing the <1>Server</1>'s underlying Listener(s).
Go to Server.ListenAndServe
func (*Server) <1>ListenAndServe</1> <2>¶</2>
ListenAndServe listens on the TCP network address s.Addr and then calls <1>Serve</1> to handle requests on incoming connections. Accepted connections are configured to enable TCP keep-alives.
If s.Addr is blank, ":http" is used.
ListenAndServe always returns a non-nil error. After <1>Server.Shutdown</1> or <2>Server.Close</2>, the returned error is <3>ErrServerClosed</3>.
Go to Server.ListenAndServeTLS
func (*Server) <1>ListenAndServeTLS</1> <2>¶</2>
ListenAndServeTLS listens on the TCP network address s.Addr and then calls <1>ServeTLS</1> to handle requests on incoming TLS connections. Accepted connections are configured to enable TCP keep-alives.
Filenames containing a certificate and matching private key for the server must be provided if neither the <1>Server</1>'s TLSConfig.Certificates nor TLSConfig.GetCertificate are populated. If the certificate is signed by a certificate authority, the certFile should be the concatenation of the server's certificate, any intermediates, and the CA's certificate.
If s.Addr is blank, ":https" is used.
ListenAndServeTLS always returns a non-nil error. After <1>Server.Shutdown</1> or <2>Server.Close</2>, the returned error is <3>ErrServerClosed</3>.
Go to Server.RegisterOnShutdown
func (*Server) <1>RegisterOnShutdown</1> <2>¶</2>
RegisterOnShutdown registers a function to call on <1>Server.Shutdown</1>. This can be used to gracefully shutdown connections that have undergone ALPN protocol upgrade or that have been hijacked. This function should start protocol-specific graceful shutdown, but should not wait for shutdown to complete.
Go to Server.Serve
func (*Server) <1>Serve</1> <2>¶</2>
Serve accepts incoming connections on the Listener l, creating a new service goroutine for each. The service goroutines read requests and then call s.Handler to reply to them.
HTTP/2 support is only enabled if the Listener returns <1>*tls.Conn</1> connections and they were configured with "h2" in the TLS Config.NextProtos.
Serve always returns a non-nil error and closes l. After <1>Server.Shutdown</1> or <2>Server.Close</2>, the returned error is <3>ErrServerClosed</3>.
Go to Server.ServeTLS
func (*Server) <1>ServeTLS</1> <2>¶</2>
ServeTLS accepts incoming connections on the Listener l, creating a new service goroutine for each. The service goroutines perform TLS setup and then read requests, calling s.Handler to reply to them.
Files containing a certificate and matching private key for the server must be provided if neither the <1>Server</1>'s TLSConfig.Certificates, TLSConfig.GetCertificate nor config.GetConfigForClient are populated. If the certificate is signed by a certificate authority, the certFile should be the concatenation of the server's certificate, any intermediates, and the CA's certificate.
ServeTLS always returns a non-nil error. After <1>Server.Shutdown</1> or <2>Server.Close</2>, the returned error is <3>ErrServerClosed</3>.
Go to Server.SetKeepAlivesEnabled
func (*Server) <1>SetKeepAlivesEnabled</1> <2>¶</2>
SetKeepAlivesEnabled controls whether HTTP keep-alives are enabled. By default, keep-alives are always enabled. Only very resource-constrained environments or servers in the process of shutting down should disable them.
Go to Server.Shutdown
func (*Server) <1>Shutdown</1> <2>¶</2>
Shutdown gracefully shuts down the server without interrupting any active connections. Shutdown works by first closing all open listeners, then closing all idle connections, and then waiting indefinitely for connections to return to idle and then shut down. If the provided context expires before the shutdown is complete, Shutdown returns the context's error, otherwise it returns any error returned from closing the <1>Server</1>'s underlying Listener(s).
When Shutdown is called, <1>Serve</1>, <2>ServeTLS</2>, <3>ListenAndServe</3>, and <4>ListenAndServeTLS</4> immediately return <5>ErrServerClosed</5>. Make sure the program doesn't exit and waits instead for Shutdown to return.
Shutdown does not attempt to close nor wait for hijacked connections such as WebSockets. The caller of Shutdown should separately notify such long-lived connections of shutdown and wait for them to close, if desired. See <1>Server.RegisterOnShutdown</1> for a way to register shutdown notification functions.
Once Shutdown has been called on a server, it may not be reused; future calls to methods such as Serve will return ErrServerClosed.
Go to Transport
type <1>Transport</1> <2>¶</2>
Transport is an implementation of <1>RoundTripper</1> that supports HTTP, HTTPS, and HTTP proxies (for either HTTP or HTTPS with CONNECT).
By default, Transport caches connections for future re-use. This may leave many open connections when accessing many hosts. This behavior can be managed using <1>Transport.CloseIdleConnections</1> method and the <2>Transport.MaxIdleConnsPerHost</2> and <3>Transport.DisableKeepAlives</3> fields.
Transports should be reused instead of created as needed. Transports are safe for concurrent use by multiple goroutines.
A Transport is a low-level primitive for making HTTP and HTTPS requests. For high-level functionality, such as cookies and redirects, see <1>Client</1>.
Transport uses HTTP/1.1 for HTTP URLs and either HTTP/1.1 or HTTP/2 for HTTPS URLs, depending on whether the server supports HTTP/2, and how the Transport is configured. The <1>DefaultTransport</1> supports HTTP/2. To explicitly enable HTTP/2 on a transport, set <2>Transport.Protocols</2>.
Responses with status codes in the 1xx range are either handled automatically (100 expect-continue) or ignored. The one exception is HTTP status code 101 (Switching Protocols), which is considered a terminal status and returned by <1>Transport.RoundTrip</1>. To see the ignored 1xx responses, use the httptrace trace package's ClientTrace.Got1xxResponse.
Transport only retries a request upon encountering a network error if the connection has already been used successfully and if the request is idempotent and either has no body or has its <1>Request.GetBody</1> defined. HTTP requests are considered idempotent if they have HTTP methods GET, HEAD, OPTIONS, or TRACE; or if their <2>Header</2> map contains an "Idempotency-Key" or "X-Idempotency-Key" entry. If the idempotency key value is a zero-length slice, the request is treated as idempotent but the header is not sent on the wire.
func (*Transport)
CancelRequest cancels an in-flight request by closing its connection. CancelRequest should only be called after <1>Transport.RoundTrip</1> has returned.
Deprecated: Use <1>Request.WithContext</1> to create a request with a cancelable context instead. CancelRequest cannot cancel HTTP/2 requests. This may become a no-op in a future release of Go.
Go to Transport.Clone
func (*Transport) <1>Clone</1> <2>¶</2>
Clone returns a deep copy of t's exported fields.
Go to Transport.CloseIdleConnections
func (*Transport) <1>CloseIdleConnections</1> <2>¶</2>
CloseIdleConnections closes any connections which were previously connected from previous requests but are now sitting idle in a "keep-alive" state. It does not interrupt any connections currently in use.
Go to Transport.NewClientConn
func (*Transport) <1>NewClientConn</1> <2>¶</2>
NewClientConn creates a new client connection to the given address.
If scheme is "http", the connection is unencrypted. If scheme is "https", the connection uses TLS.
The protocol used for the new connection is determined by the scheme, Transport.Protocols configuration field, and protocols supported by the server. See Transport.Protocols for more details.
If Transport.Proxy is set and indicates that a request sent to the given address should use a proxy, the new connection uses that proxy.
NewClientConn always creates a new connection, even if the Transport has an existing cached connection to the given host.
The new connection is not added to the Transport's connection cache, and will not be used by <1>Transport.RoundTrip</1>. It does not count against the MaxIdleConns and MaxConnsPerHost limits.
The caller is responsible for closing the new connection.
Go to Transport.RegisterProtocol
func (*Transport) <1>RegisterProtocol</1> <2>¶</2>
RegisterProtocol registers a new protocol with scheme. The <1>Transport</1> will pass requests using the given scheme to rt. It is rt's responsibility to simulate HTTP request semantics.
RegisterProtocol can be used by other packages to provide implementations of protocol schemes like "ftp" or "file".
If rt.RoundTrip returns <1>ErrSkipAltProtocol</1>, the Transport will handle the <2>Transport.RoundTrip</2> itself for that one request, as if the protocol were not registered.
Go to Transport.RoundTrip
func (*Transport) <1>RoundTrip</1> <2>¶</2>
For higher-level HTTP client support (such as handling of cookies and redirects), see <1>Get</1>, <2>Post</2>, and the <3>Client</3> type.
Like the RoundTripper interface, the error types returned by RoundTrip are unspecified.

Extensions / Premiers pas | Get started | Chrome for Developers
{1|} <2> Google utilise la technologie IA pour traduire le contenu dans votre langue préférée. Les traductions générées par IA peuvent contenir des erreurs. </2>
Premiers pas
Bienvenue dans le développement d'extensions Chrome. Découvrez tout ce dont vous avez besoin pour commencer à créer et à distribuer votre première extension Chrome.
Créer votre première extension
Voir tous les tutoriels
Aperçu
Que sont les extensions ?
Les extensions Chrome améliorent l'expérience de navigation en personnalisant l'interface utilisateur, en observant les événements du navigateur et en modifiant le Web. Consultez le <1>Chrome Web Store</1> pour découvrir d'autres exemples de ce que les extensions peuvent faire.
Comment sont-elles créées ?
Vous pouvez créer des extensions à l'aide des mêmes technologies Web que celles utilisées pour créer des applications Web: <1>HTML</1>, <2>CSS</2> et <3>JavaScript</3>.
Que peut-il faire ?
En plus des <1>API Web</1>, les extensions ont également accès aux <2>API d'extension Chrome</2> pour effectuer différentes tâches. Pour en savoir plus, consultez le <3>guide de développement</3>.
Terminologie des extensions
Chrome for Developers

Chrome Web Store
Menu déroulant de Chrome Web Store
Onglets inférieurs
Sur cette page
Une extension Chrome est composée de parties qui jouent différents rôles.
article
<1> Fichier manifeste </1>
Le fichier manifeste de l'extension est le seul fichier obligatoire qui doit porter un nom de fichier spécifique: manifest.json. Il doit également se trouver dans le répertoire racine de l'extension. Le fichier manifeste enregistre des métadonnées importantes, définit des ressources, déclare des autorisations et identifie les fichiers à exécuter en arrière-plan et sur la page.
<1> Service workers </1>
Un service worker s'exécute en arrière-plan et gère les événements du navigateur, comme la suppression d'un favori ou la fermeture d'un onglet. Ils n'ont pas accès au DOM, mais vous pouvez les combiner à un document hors écran pour ce cas d'utilisation.
<1> Scripts de contenu </1>
Les scripts de contenu exécutent JavaScript dans le contexte d'une page Web.
<1> Action de la barre d'outils </1>
Exécutez du code lorsque l'utilisateur clique sur l'icône de la barre d'outils de l'extension ou affichez une fenêtre pop-up à l'aide de l'API Action.
<1> Panneau latéral </1>
Affichez une UI personnalisée dans le panneau latéral du navigateur.
<1> DeclarativeNetRequest </1>
Intercepter, bloquer ou modifier des requêtes réseau
Publier sur le Chrome Web Store
Si vous créez l'extension vous-même, consultez notre <1>tutoriel de démarrage</1>. Si vous souhaitez publier une application sur le Chrome Web Store, vous devez d'abord connaître quelques points importants.
palette
Concevoir une extension de haute qualité
Lorsque vous choisissez les fonctionnalités à prendre en charge, assurez-vous que votre extension a un <1>seul objectif</1>, qui est défini de manière précise et facile à comprendre.
En savoir plus
build
Familiarisez-vous avec les règles
Les extensions distribuées sur le Chrome Web Store doivent respecter le <1>Règlement du programme pour les développeurs</1>. Consultez ces règles pour vous assurer que votre extension peut être hébergée sur le Chrome Web Store.
Inclure toute la logique de l'extension
Lorsque vous écrivez votre code, n'oubliez pas que toute logique doit être incluse dans le package d'extension. Cela signifie qu'aucun code JavaScript supplémentaire ne peut être téléchargé au moment de l'exécution. <1>Améliorer la sécurité des extensions</1> propose des alternatives à l'exécution du code hébergé à distance.

Choisissez l'un des tutoriels suivants pour commencer votre parcours de formation complémentaire.
code

<1>JavaScript</1> (<2>JS</2>)<3><4><5><6>[</6>a<7>]</7></5></4></3> is a <8>programming language</8> and core technology of <9>the Web</9>, alongside <10>HTML</10> and <11>CSS</11>. Created by <12>Brendan Eich</12> in 1995,<13><14><15><16>[</16>6<17>]</17></15></14></13> it is maintained by <18>Ecma International</18>'s <19>TC39</19> technical committee,<20><21><22><23>[</23>10<24>]</24></22></21></20> with related <25>Web APIs</25> maintained by <26>W3C</26> and <27>WHATWG</27>.<28><29><30><31>[</31>11<32>]</32></30></29></28> In 2025, JavaScript was the most widely used programming language on <33>GitHub</33>.<34><35><36><37>[</37>b<38>]</38></36></35></34><39><40><41><42>[</42>12<43>]</43></41></40></39>
Web browser
JavaScript engine
Source code
Server (computing)
Application software
Runtime system
<1>Web browsers</1> have a dedicated <2>JavaScript engine</2> that executes the client <3>code</3>. These engines are also utilized in some <4>servers</4> and a variety of <5>apps</5>. The most popular <6>runtime system</6> for non-browser usage is <7>Node.js</7>.<8><9><10><11>[</11>13<12>]</12></10></9></8>
High-level programming language
Just-in-time compilation
Prototype-based programming
First-class function
Application programming interface
Regular expression
Data structure
Document Object Model
JavaScript is a <1>high-level</1>, often <2>just-in-time–compiled</2> language that conforms to the <3>ECMAScript</3> standard.<4><5><6><7>[</7>14<8>]</8></6></5></4> It has <9>dynamic typing</9>, <10>prototype-based</10> <11>object-orientation</11>, and <12>first-class functions</12>. It is <13>multi-paradigm</13>, supporting <14>event-driven</14>, <15>functional</15>, and <16>imperative</16> programming styles. It has <17>application programming interfaces</17> (APIs) for working with text, dates, <18>regular expressions</18>, standard <19>data structures</19>, and the <20>Document Object Model</20> (DOM).
Computer network
Data storage
Computer graphics
The ECMAScript standard does not include any <1>input/output</1> (I/O), such as <2>networking</2>, <3>storage</3>, or <4>graphics</4> facilities. In practice, the web browser or other runtime system provides JavaScript APIs for I/O.
Syntax (programming languages)
Although <1>Java</1> and JavaScript are similar in name and <2>syntax</2>, the two languages are distinct and differ greatly in design.
<1>History</1>
<1>Creation at Netscape</1>
Graphical user interface
Mosaic (web browser)
Netscape Navigator
The first popular <1>web browser</1> with a <2>graphical user interface</2>, <3>Mosaic</3>, was released in 1993. The lead developers of Mosaic then founded the <4>Netscape</4> corporation, which released a more polished browser, <5>Netscape Navigator</5>, in 1994. This quickly became the most-used.<6><7><8><9>[</9>15<10>]</10></8></7></6>
Web page
Sun Microsystems
During these formative years of the Web, <1>web pages</1> could only be static, lacking the capability for dynamic behavior after the page was loaded in the browser. There was a desire in the flourishing web development scene to remove this limitation, so in 1995, Netscape decided to add a <2>programming language</2> to Navigator. They pursued two routes to achieve this: collaborating with <3>Sun Microsystems</3> to embed the <4>Java</4> language, while also hiring <5>Brendan Eich</5> to embed the <6>Scheme</6> language.<7><8><9><10>[</10>6<11>]</11></9></8></7>

<1>Java</1>,<2><3><4><5>[</5>5<6>]</6></4></3></2><7><8><9><10>[</10>6<11>]</11></9></8></7> <12>Scheme</12>,<13><14><15><16>[</16>6<17>]</17></15></14></13> <18>Self</18>,<19><20><21><22>[</22>7<23>]</23></21></20></19> <24>AWK</24>,<25><26><27><28>[</28>8<29>]</29></27></26></25> <30>HyperTalk</30><31><32><33><34>[</34>9<35>]</35></33></32></31>
Influenced
Dart (programming language)
Haxe
JS++
Opa (programming language)
<1>ActionScript</1>, <2>ArkTS</2>, <3>AssemblyScript</3>, <4>CoffeeScript</4>, <5>Dart</5>, <6>Haxe</6>, <7>JS++</7>, <8>Opa</8>, <9>TypeScript</9>
<1>{2|} <3>JavaScript</3> at Wikibooks</1>
Scripting language
Interpreter (computing)
Beta software
The goal was a "language for the masses",<1><2><3><4>[</4>16<5>]</5></3></2></1> "to help nonprogrammers create dynamic, interactive Web sites".<6><7><8><9>[</9>17<10>]</10></8></7></6> Netscape management soon decided that the best option was for Eich to devise a new language, with syntax similar to Java and less like Scheme or other extant <11>scripting languages</11>.<12><13><14><15>[</15>5<16>]</16></14></13></12><17><18><19><20>[</20>6<21>]</21></19></18></17> Although the new language and its <22>interpreter</22> implementation were called LiveScript when first shipped as part of a Navigator <23>beta</23> in September 1995, the name was changed to JavaScript for the official release in December.<24><25><26><27>[</27>1<28>]</28></26></25></24><29><30><31><32>[</32>6<33>]</33></31></30></29><34><35><36><37>[</37>18<38>]</38></36></35></34><39><40><41><42>[</42>19<43>]</43></41></40></39>
Dot-com bubble
The choice of the <1>JavaScript</1> name has caused confusion, implying that it is directly related to Java. At the time, the <2>dot-com boom</2> had begun and Java was a popular new language, so Eich considered the name a marketing ploy by Netscape.<3><4><5><6>[</6>16<7>]</7></5></4></3>
<1>Adoption by Microsoft</1>
Internet Explorer
Browser war
JScript
<1>Microsoft</1> debuted <2>Internet Explorer</2> in 1995, leading to a <3>browser war</3> with Netscape. On the JavaScript front, Microsoft created its own <4>interpreter</4> called <5>JScript</5>.<6><7><8><9>[</9>20<10>]</10></8></7></6>
Implementation
Microsoft first released JScript in 1996, alongside initial support for <1>CSS</1> and extensions to <2>HTML</2>. Each of these <3>implementations</3> was noticeably different from their counterparts in <4>Netscape Navigator</4>.<5><6><7><8>[</8>21<9>]</9></7></6></5><10><11><12><13>[</13>22<14>]</14></12></11></10> These differences made it difficult for developers to make their websites work well in both browsers, leading to widespread use of "best viewed in Netscape" and "best viewed in Internet Explorer" logos for several years.<15><16><17><18>[</18>21<19>]</19></17></16></15><20><21><22><23>[</23>23<24>]</24></22></21></20>
<1>The rise of JScript</1>
Sidekick
Pop-up ad
Status bar
<1>Brendan Eich</1> later said of this period: "It's still kind of a <2>sidekick</2> language. It's considered slow or annoying. People do <3>pop-ups</3> or those scrolling messages in the old <4>status bar</4> at the bottom of your old <5>browser</5>."<6><7><8><9>[</9>16<10>]</10></8></7></6>
In November 1996, <1>Netscape</1> submitted JavaScript to <2>Ecma International</2>, as the starting point for a standard specification that all browser vendors could conform to. This led to the official release of the first <3>ECMAScript</3> language specification in June 1997.
The standards process continued for a few years, with the release of ECMAScript 2 in June 1998 and ECMAScript 3 in December 1999. Work on ECMAScript 4 began in 2000.<1><2><3><4>[</4>20<5>]</5></3></2></1>
Client-side scripting
However, the effort to fully standardize the language was undermined by Microsoft gaining an increasingly dominant position in the browser market. By the early 2000s, <1>Internet Explorer</1>'s market share reached 95%.<2><3><4><5>[</5>24<6>]</6></4></3></2> This meant that <7>JScript</7> became the de facto standard for <8>client-side scripting</8> on the Web.
Microsoft initially participated in the standards process and implemented some proposals in its JScript language, but eventually it stopped collaborating on ECMA work. Thus ECMAScript 4 was mothballed.
<1>Growth and standardization</1>
Mozilla
Firefox
During the period of <1>Internet Explorer</1> dominance in the early 2000s, client-side scripting was stagnant. This started to change in 2004, when the successor of Netscape, <2>Mozilla</2>, released the <3>Firefox</3> browser. Firefox was well received by many, taking significant market share from Internet Explorer.<4><5><6><7>[</7>25<8>]</8></6></5></4>
ECMAScript for XML
Macromedia
Adobe Systems
Tamarin (software)
Open-source model
In 2005, Mozilla joined ECMA International, and work started on the <1>ECMAScript for XML</1> (E4X) standard. This led to Mozilla working jointly with <2>Macromedia</2> (later acquired by <3>Adobe Systems</3>), who were implementing E4X in their ActionScript 3 language, which was based on an ECMAScript 4 draft. The goal became standardizing ActionScript 3 as the new ECMAScript 4. To this end, Adobe Systems released the <4>Tamarin</4> implementation as an <5>open source</5> project. However, Tamarin and ActionScript 3 were too different from established client-side scripting, and without cooperation from Microsoft, ECMAScript 4 never reached fruition.
Jesse James Garrett
Ajax (programming)
Web application
JQuery
Prototype JavaScript Framework
Dojo Toolkit
Meanwhile, very important developments were occurring in open-source communities not affiliated with ECMA work. In 2005, <1>Jesse James Garrett</1> released a white paper in which he coined the term <2>Ajax</2> and described a set of technologies, of which JavaScript was the backbone, to create <3>web applications</3> where data can be loaded in the background, avoiding the need for full page reloads. This sparked a renaissance period of JavaScript, spearheaded by open-source libraries and the communities that formed around them. Many new libraries were created, including <4>jQuery</4>, <5>Prototype</5>, <6>Dojo Toolkit</6>, and <7>MooTools</7>.
Google
Google Chrome
<1>Google</1> debuted its <2>Chrome</2> browser in 2008, with the <3>V8</3> JavaScript engine that was faster than its competition.<4><5><6><7>[</7>26<8>]</8></6></5></4><9><10><11><12>[</12>27<13>]</13></11></10></9> The key innovation was <14>just-in-time compilation</14> (JIT),<15><16><17><18>[</18>28<19>]</19></17></16></15> so other browser vendors needed to overhaul their engines for JIT.<20><21><22><23>[</23>29<24>]</24></22></21></20>
Oslo
In July 2008, these disparate parties came together for a conference in <1>Oslo</1>. This led to the eventual agreement in early 2009 to combine all relevant work and drive the language forward. The result was the ECMAScript 5 standard, released in December 2009.
<1>Reaching maturity</1>
Chart of the most popular programming languages on <1>GitHub</1>
ECMAScript 6
Ambitious work on the language continued for several years, culminating in an extensive collection of additions and refinements being formalized with the publication of <1>ECMAScript 6</1> in 2015.<2><3><4><5>[</5>30<6>]</6></4></3></2>
Ryan Dahl
Event loop
Npm (software)
Package manager
The creation of <1>Node.js</1> in 2009 by <2>Ryan Dahl</2> sparked a significant increase in the usage of JavaScript outside of web browsers. Node combines the <3>V8</3> engine, an <4>event loop</4>, and <5>I/O</5> <6>APIs</6>, thereby providing a stand-alone JavaScript runtime system.<7><8><9><10>[</10>31<11>]</11></9></8></7><12><13><14><15>[</15>32<16>]</16></14></13></12> As of 2018, Node had been used by millions of developers,<17><18><19><20>[</20>33<21>]</21></19></18></17> and <22>npm</22> had the most modules of any <23>package manager</23> in the world.<24><25><26><27>[</27>34<28>]</28></26></25></24>
The ECMAScript draft specification is currently maintained openly on <1>GitHub</1>,<2><3><4><5>[</5>35<6>]</6></4></3></2> and editions are produced via regular annual snapshots.<7><8><9><10>[</10>35<11>]</11></9></8></7> Potential revisions to the language are vetted through a comprehensive proposal process.<12><13><14><15>[</15>36<16>]</16></14></13></12><17><18><19><20>[</20>37<21>]</21></19></18></17> Now, instead of edition numbers, developers check the status of upcoming features individually.<22><23><24><25>[</25>35<26>]</26></24></23></22>
List of JavaScript libraries
Web framework
Single-page application
Source-to-source compiler
The current JavaScript ecosystem has many <1>libraries</1> and <2>frameworks</2>, established programming practices, and substantial usage of JavaScript outside of web browsers.<3><4><5><6>[</6>19<7>]</7></5></4></3> Plus, with the rise of <8>single-page applications</8> and other JavaScript-heavy websites, several <9>transpilers</9> have been created to aid the development process.<10><11><12><13>[</13>38<14>]</14></12></11></10>
Client (computing)
As of 2025, 98.9% of <1>websites</1> use JavaScript for <2>client</2> side <3>webpage</3> behavior.<4><5><6><7>[</7>39<8>]</8></6></5></4>
Trademark
Oracle Corporation
Acquisition of Sun Microsystems by Oracle Corporation
"JavaScript" is a <1>trademark</1> of <2>Oracle Corporation</2> in the United States.<3><4><5><6>[</6>40<7>]</7></5></4></3><8><9><10><11>[</11>41<12>]</12></10></9></8> The trademark was originally issued to <13>Sun Microsystems</13> on 6 May 1997, and was transferred to Oracle when <14>they acquired Sun</14> in 2009.<15><16><17><18>[</18>42<19>]</19></17></16></15><20><21><22><23>[</23>43<24>]</24></22></21></20>
A letter was circulated in September 2024, spearheaded by <1>Ryan Dahl</1>, calling on Oracle to free the JavaScript trademark.<2><3><4><5>[</5>44<6>]</6></4></3></2> <7>Brendan Eich</7>, the original creator of JavaScript, was among the over 14,000 signatories who supported the initiative.
Client-side
JavaScript is the dominant <1>client-side</1> <2>scripting language</2> of the Web, with 98.9% of all <3>websites</3> using it for this purpose.<4><5><6><7>[</7>39<8>]</8></6></5></4> Scripts are embedded in or included from <9>HTML</9> documents and can interact with the <10>DOM</10> and other web APIs.
All major <1>web browsers</1> have a built-in <2>JavaScript engine</2> that executes the <3>code</3> on the user's device.
<1>Examples of scripted behavior</1>
Clipboard (computing)
<1>Copy to clipboard</1> functionality.<2><3><4><5>[</5>45<6>]</6></4></3></2>
HTTP requests
User (computing)
Social media
Sending <1>HTTP requests</1> and/or loading new <2>web page</2> content without reloading the page, via <3>Ajax</3> or a <4>WebSocket</4>. For example, <5>users</5> of <6>social media</6> can send and receive messages without leaving the current page.
Web page animations, such as fading objects in and out, resizing, and moving them.
Browser game
<1>Browser games</1>.
Media player software
Streaming media
Controlling the <1>playback</1> of <2>streaming media</2>.
Generating <1>pop-up ads</1> or alert boxes.
Data validation
Form (HTML)
Web server
<1>Validating</1> input values of a <2>web form</2> before the data is sent to a <3>web server</3>.
Web analytics
Ad tracking
Personalization
Logging data about the user's behavior then sending it to a server. The website owner can use this data for <1>analytics</1>, <2>ad tracking</2>, and <3>personalization</3>.
Web storage
Indexed Database API
HTTP cookie
Storing and retrieving data on the user's device, typically using the <1>web storage</1> or <2>IndexedDB</2> APIs (and sometimes <3>cookies</3>, although those can be set/read without JavaScript).
<1>Libraries and frameworks</1>
Library (computing)
Over 80% of websites use a third-party JavaScript <1>library</1> or <2>web framework</2> as part of their client-side scripting.<3><4><5><6>[</6>46<7>]</7></5></4></3>
Angular (web framework)
Bootstrap (front-end framework)
Lodash
Modernizr
React (software)
<1>jQuery</1> is by far the most-used.<2><3><4><5>[</5>46<6>]</6></4></3></2> Other notable ones include <7>Angular</7>, <8>Bootstrap</8>, <9>Lodash</9>, <10>Modernizr</10>, <11>React</11>, <12>Underscore</12>, and <13>Vue</13>.<14><15><16><17>[</17>46<18>]</18></16></15></14> Multiple options can be used in conjunction, such as jQuery and Bootstrap.<19><20><21><22>[</22>47<23>]</23></21></20></19>
The term "Vanilla JS" was coined for websites not using any libraries or frameworks at all, instead relying entirely on standard JavaScript functionality.<1><2><3><4>[</4>48<5>]</5></3></2></1>
<1>Other usage{2|}{3|}</1>
Server-side
The use of JavaScript has expanded beyond its <1>web browser</1> roots. <2>JavaScript engines</2> are now embedded in a variety of other software systems, both for <3>server-side</3> website deployments and non-browser <4>applications</4>.
Netscape Enterprise Server
Internet Information Services
List of server-side JavaScript implementations
Initial attempts at promoting server-side JavaScript usage were <1>Netscape Enterprise Server</1> and <2>Microsoft</2>'s <3>Internet Information Services</3>,<4><5><6><7>[</7>49<8>]</8></6></5></4><9><10><11><12>[</12>50<13>]</13></11></10></9> but they were small niches.<14><15><16><17>[</17>51<18>]</18></16></15></14> Server-side usage eventually started to grow in the late 2000s, with the creation of <19>Node.js</19> and <20>other approaches</20>.<21><22><23><24>[</24>51<25>]</25></23></22></21>
Electron (software framework)
Apache Cordova
Application framework
Adobe Acrobat
PDF
GNOME Shell
<1>Electron</1>, <2>Cordova</2>, <3>React Native</3>, and other <4>application frameworks</4> have been used to create many applications with behavior implemented in JavaScript. Other non-browser applications include <5>Adobe Acrobat</5> support for scripting <6>PDF</6> documents<7><8><9><10>[</10>52<11>]</11></9></8></7> and <12>GNOME Shell</12> extensions written in JavaScript.<13><14><15><16>[</16>53<17>]</17></15></14></13>
Nashorn (JavaScript engine)
Java Development Kit
<1>Oracle</1> used to provide <2>Nashorn</2>, a JavaScript interpreter, as part of their <3>Java Development Kit</3> (JDK) API library along with <4>jjs</4> a command line interpreter as of JDK version 8. It was removed in JDK 15. As a replacement Oracle offered GraalJS which can also be used with the <5>OpenJDK</5> which allows one to create and reference Java objects in JavaScript code and add runtime scripting in JavaScript to applications written in Java.<6><7><8><9>[</9>54<10>]</10></8></7></6><11><12><13><14>[</14>55<15>]</15></13></12></11><16><17><18><19>[</19>56<20>]</20></18></17></16><21><22><23><24>[</24>57<25>]</25></23></22></21>
Embedded system
JavaScript has been used in some <1>embedded systems</1>, usually by leveraging Node.js.<2><3><4><5>[</5>58<6>]</6></4></3></2><7><8><9><10>[</10>59<11>]</11></9></8></7><12><13><14><15>[</15>60<16>]</16></14></13></12>
<1>JavaScript engine</1>
List of JavaScript engines
This section is an excerpt from <1>List of JavaScript engines</1>.
edit
Software engine
Browser engine
Web IDL
Sandbox (computer security)
The first JavaScript <1>engines</1> were <2>interpreters</2> of the <3>source code</3>, but modern engines use <4>just-in-time compilation</4> to improve performance.<5><6><7><8>[</8>61<9>]</9></7></6></5> JavaScript engines are typically developed by <10>web browser</10> vendors, and every major browser includes one. In a browser, the JavaScript engine runs in concert with the <11>rendering engine</11> via the <12>Document Object Model</12> and <13>Web IDL</13> bindings.<14><15><16><17>[</17>62<18>]</18></16></15></14> However, the use of JavaScript engines is not limited to browsers; For example, the <19>V8 engine</19> is a core component of the <20>Node.js</20> <21>runtime system</21>.<22><23><24><25>[</25>63<26>]</26></24></23></22> They are also called <27>ECMAScript</27> engines, after the official name of the specification. With the advent of <28>WebAssembly</28>, some engines can also execute this code in the same <29>sandbox</29> as regular JavaScript code.<30><31><32><33>[</33>64<34>]</34></32></31></30><35><36><37><38>[</38>63<39>]</39></37></36></35>
<1>Runtime system</1>
A JavaScript engine must be embedded within a <1>runtime system</1> (such as a <2>web browser</2> or a standalone system) to enable scripts to interact with the broader environment. The runtime system includes the necessary APIs for <3>input/output</3> operations, such as <4>networking</4>, <5>storage</5>, and <6>graphics</6>, and provides the ability to import scripts.
Thread (computing)
Message (computer science)
Queue (abstract data type)
Subroutine
Call stack
Parameter (computer programming)
Local variable
Concurrency (computer science)
Asynchronous I/O
Event (computing)
Callback (computer programming)
JavaScript is a single-<1>threaded</1> language. The runtime processes <2>messages</2> from a <3>queue</3> one at a time, and it calls a <4>function</4> associated with each new message, creating a <5>call stack</5> frame with the function's <6>arguments</6> and <7>local variables</7>. The call stack shrinks and grows based on the function's needs. When the call stack is empty upon function completion, JavaScript proceeds to the next message in the queue. This is called the <8>event loop</8>, described as "run to completion" because each message is fully processed before the next message is considered. However, the language's <9>concurrency model</9> describes the event loop as <10>non-blocking</10>: program I/O is performed using <11>events</11> and <12>callback functions</12>. This means, for example, that JavaScript can process a mouse click while waiting for a database query to return information.<13><14><15><16>[</16>65<17>]</17></15></14></13>
Deno (software)
Bun (software)
Notable standalone runtimes are <1>Node.js</1>, <2>Deno</2>, and <3>Bun</3>.
The following features are common to all conforming ECMAScript implementations unless explicitly specified otherwise. The number of cited reserved words including keywords is 50–60 and varies depending on the implementation.
<1>Imperative and structured</1>
Structured programming
Main article: <1>Structured programming</1>
C (computer language)
Function scoping
Block scoping
Expression (computer science)
Statement (computer science)
Defensive semicolon
JavaScript supports much of the <1>structured programming</1> syntax from <2>C</2> (e.g., {3|if} statements, {4|while} loops, {5|switch} statements, {6|do while} loops, etc.). One partial exception is <7>scoping</7>: originally JavaScript only had <8>function scoping</8> with {9|var}; <10>block scoping</10> was added in ECMAScript 2015 with the keywords {11|let} and {12|const}. Like C, JavaScript makes a distinction between <13>expressions</13> and <14>statements</14>. One syntactic difference from C is <15>automatic semicolon insertion</15>, which allow semicolons (which terminate statements) to be omitted.<16><17><18><19>[</19>66<20>]</20></18></17></16>
<1>Weakly typed</1>
Weakly typed
Further information: <1>Weakly typed</1>
JavaScript is <1>weakly typed</1>, which means certain types are implicitly cast depending on the operation used.<2><3><4><5>[</5>67<6>]</6></4></3></2>
The binary {1|+} operator casts both operands to a string unless both operands are numbers. This is because the addition operator doubles as a concatenation operator
The binary {1|-} operator always casts both operands to a number
Double-precision floating-point format
Arbitrary-precision arithmetic
Both unary operators ({1|+}, {2|-}) always cast the operand to a number. However, {3|+} always casts to {4|Number} (<5>binary64</5>) while {6|-} preserves {7|BigInt} (<8>integer</8>)<9><10><11><12>[</12>68<13>]</13></11></10></9>
Values are cast to strings as follows:<1><2><3><4>[</4>67<5>]</5></3></2></1>
Strings are left as-is
Numbers are converted to their string representation
Arrays have their elements cast to strings after which they are joined by commas ({1|,})
Other objects are converted to the string {1|[object Object]} where {2|Object} is the name of the constructor of the object
Values are cast to numbers by casting to strings and then casting the strings to numbers. These processes can be modified by defining {1|toString} and {2|valueOf} functions on the <3>prototype</3> for string and number casting respectively.
JavaScript has received criticism for the way it implements these conversions as the complexity of the rules can be mistaken for inconsistency.<1><2><3><4>[</4>69<5>]</5></3></2></1><6><7><8><9>[</9>67<10>]</10></8></7></6> For example, when adding a number to a string, the number will be cast to a string before performing concatenation, but when subtracting a number from a string, the string is cast to a number before performing subtraction.
JavaScript type conversions
left operand
operator
right operand
result
{1|[]} (empty array)
{1|""} (empty string)
{1|&#123;&#125;} (empty object)
{1|"[object Object]"} (string)
{1|false} (boolean)
{1|"false"} (string)
{1|"123"}(string)
{1|1} (number)
{1|"1231"} (string)
{1|"123"} (string)
{1|122} (number)
{1|"abc"} (string)
{1|NaN} (number)
Often also mentioned is {1|&#123;&#125; + []} resulting in {2|0} (number). This is misleading: the {3|&#123;&#125;} is interpreted as an empty code block instead of an empty object, and the empty array is cast to a number by the remaining unary {4|+} operator. If the expression is wrapped in parentheses - {5|(&#123;&#125; + [])} – the curly brackets are interpreted as an empty object and the result of the expression is {6|"[object Object]"} as expected.<7><8><9><10>[</10>67<11>]</11></9></8></7>
<1>Dynamic</1>
Dynamic programming
Main article: <1>Dynamic programming</1>
<1>Typing</1>
Main article: <1>Dynamic typing</1>
Value (computer science)
Variable (programming)
String (computer science)
JavaScript is <1>dynamically typed</1> like most other <2>scripting languages</2>. A <3>type</3> is associated with a <4>value</4> rather than an expression. For example, a <5>variable</5> initially bound to a number may be reassigned to a <6>string</6>.<7><8><9><10>[</10>70<11>]</11></9></8></7> JavaScript supports various ways to test the type of objects, including <12>duck typing</12>.<13><14><15><16>[</16>71<17>]</17></15></14></13>
<1>Run-time evaluation</1>
Eval
Main article: <1>eval</1>
JavaScript includes an {1|eval} function that can execute statements provided as strings at run-time.
<1>{2|}Object-orientation (prototype-based)</1>
Douglas Crockford
Prototypal inheritance in JavaScript is described by <1>Douglas Crockford</1> as:
You make prototype objects, and then ... make new instances. Objects are mutable in JavaScript, so we can augment the new instances, giving them new fields and methods. These can then act as prototypes for even newer objects. We don't need classes to make lots of similar objects... Objects inherit from objects. What could be more object oriented than that?<1><2><3><4>[</4>72<5>]</5></3></2></1>
Object (computer science)
Associative array
Property (programming)
In JavaScript, an <1>object</1> is an <2>associative array</2>, augmented with a prototype (see below); each key provides the name for an object <3>property</3>, and there are two syntactical ways to specify such a name: dot notation ({4|obj.x = 10}) and bracket notation ({5|obj["x"] = 10}). A property may be added, rebound, or deleted at run-time. Most <6>properties</6> of an object (and any property that belongs to an object's prototype inheritance chain) can be enumerated using a {7|for...in} loop.
<1>Prototypes</1>
Main article: <1>Prototype-based programming</1>
Class (computer science)
Inheritance (object-oriented programming)
JavaScript uses <1>prototypes</1> where many other object-oriented languages use <2>classes</2> for <3>inheritance</3>,<4><5><6><7>[</7>73<8>]</8></6></5></4> but it's still possible to simulate most class-based features with the prototype system.<9><10><11><12>[</12>74<13>]</13></11></10></9> Additionally, <14>ECMAScript version 6</14> (released June 2015) introduced the keywords <15>class</15>, <16>extends</16> and <17>super</17>, which serve as syntactic sugar to abstract the underlying prototypal inheritance system with a more conventional interface. Constructors are declared by specifying a method named <18>constructor</18>, and all classes are automatically subclasses of the base class Object, similarly to Java.
Number sign
Polymorphism (computer science)
Though the underlying object mechanism is still based on prototypes, the newer syntax is similar to other object oriented languages. Private variables are declared by prefixing the field name with a <1>number sign</1> (#), and <2>polymorphism</2> is not directly supported, although it can be emulated by manually calling different functions depending on the number and type of arguments provided.<3><4><5><6>[</6>75<7>]</7></5></4></3>
<1>Functions as object constructors</1>
Functions double as object constructors, along with their typical role. Prefixing a function call with <1>new</1> will create an instance of a prototype, inheriting properties and methods from the constructor (including properties from the {2|Object} prototype).<3><4><5><6>[</6>76<7>]</7></5></4></3> ECMAScript 5 offers the {8|Object.create} method, allowing explicit creation of an instance without automatically inheriting from the {9|Object} prototype (older environments can assign the prototype to {10|null}).<11><12><13><14>[</14>77<15>]</15></13></12></11> The constructor's {16|prototype} property determines the object used for the new object's internal prototype. New methods can be added by modifying the prototype of the function used as a constructor.
Monkey patch
JavaScript's built-in classes, such as {1|Array} and {2|Object}, also have prototypes that can be modified. However, it's generally considered bad practice to <3>modify built-in objects</3>, because third-party code may use or inherit methods and properties from these objects, and may not expect the prototype to be modified.<4><5><6><7>[</7>78<8>]</8></6></5></4>
<1>Functions as methods</1>
Method (computer science)
Main article: <1>Method (computer science)</1>
Unlike in many object-oriented languages, in JavaScript there is no distinction between a function definition and a <1>method</1> definition. Rather, the distinction occurs during function calling. When a function is called as a method of an object, the function's local <2>this</2> keyword is bound to that object for that invocation.
<1>Functional</1>
Main article: <1>Functional programming</1>
JavaScript <1>functions</1> are <2>first-class</2>; a function is considered to be an object.<3><4><5><6>[</6>79<7>]</7></5></4></3> As such, a function may have properties and methods, such as {8|.call()} and {9|.bind()}.<10><11><12><13>[</13>80<14>]</14></12></11></10>
<1>Lexical closure</1>
Closure (computer programming)
Main article: <1>Closure (computer programming)</1>
A <1>nested</1> function is a function defined within another function. It is created each time the outer function is invoked.
Scope (programming)
In addition, each nested function forms a <1>lexical closure</1>: the <2>lexical scope</2> of the outer function (including any constant, local variable, or argument value) becomes part of the internal state of each inner function object, even after execution of the outer function concludes.<3><4><5><6>[</6>81<7>]</7></5></4></3>
<1>Anonymous function</1>
Anonymous function
Main article: <1>Anonymous function</1>
JavaScript also supports <1>anonymous functions</1>.
<1>Delegative</1>
Delegation (object-oriented programming)
Main article: <1>Delegation (object-oriented programming)</1>
JavaScript supports implicit and explicit <1>delegation</1>.
<1>{2|}Functions as roles (Traits and Mixins)</1>
Role-oriented programming
Traits (computer science)
Mixin
Main articles: <1>Role-oriented programming</1>, <2>Traits (computer science)</2>, and <3>Mixin</3>
JavaScript natively supports various function-based implementations of <1>Role</1><2><3><4><5>[</5>82<6>]</6></4></3></2> patterns like <7>Traits</7><8><9><10><11>[</11>83<12>]</12></10></9></8><13><14><15><16>[</16>84<17>]</17></15></14></13> and <18>Mixins</18>.<19><20><21><22>[</22>85<23>]</23></21></20></19> Such a function defines additional behavior by at least one method bound to the {24|this} keyword within its {25|function} body. A Role then has to be delegated explicitly via {26|call} or {27|apply} to objects that need to feature additional behavior that is not shared via the prototype chain.
<1>Object composition and inheritance</1>
Object composition
Whereas explicit function-based delegation does cover <1>composition</1> in JavaScript, implicit delegation already happens every time the prototype chain is walked in order to, e.g., find a method that might be related to but is not directly owned by an object. Once the method is found it gets called within this object's context. Thus <2>inheritance</2> in JavaScript is covered by a delegation automatism that is bound to the prototype property of constructor functions.
<1>Miscellaneous</1>
<1>Zero-based numbering</1>
Zero-based numbering
JavaScript is a <1>zero-index</1> language.
<1>Variadic functions</1>
Variadic function
Main article: <1>Variadic function</1>
Formal parameter
Variadic functions
An indefinite number of parameters can be passed to a function. The function can access them through <1>formal parameters</1> and also through the local {2|arguments} object. <3>Variadic functions</3> can also be created by using the {4|bind} method.
<1>Array and object literals</1>
Associative arrays
Object literal
Main articles: <1>Associative arrays</1> and <2>Object literal</2>
JSON
Like in many scripting languages, arrays and objects (<1>associative arrays</1> in other languages) can each be created with a succinct shortcut syntax. In fact, these <2>literals</2> form the basis of the <3>JSON</3> data format.
<1>Regular expressions</1>
Main article: <1>Regular expression</1>
JavaScript supports <1>regular expressions</1> for text searches and manipulation.<2><3><4><5>[</5>76<6>]</6></4></3></2>
<1>Promises</1>
Futures and promises
Main article: <1>Futures and promises</1>
A built-in Promise object provides functionality for handling promises and associating handlers with an asynchronous action's eventual result. JavaScript supplies combinator methods, which allow developers to combine multiple JavaScript promises and do operations based on different scenarios. The methods introduced are: Promise.race, Promise.all, Promise.allSettled and Promise.any.
<1>{2|}Async/await</1>
Main article: <1>Async/await</1>
Async/await allows an asynchronous, non-blocking function to be structured in a way similar to an ordinary synchronous function. Asynchronous, non-blocking code can be written, with minimal overhead, structured similarly to traditional synchronous, blocking code.
<1>Vendor-specific extensions</1>
Historically, some <1>JavaScript engines</1> supported these non-standard features:
List comprehension
<1>array comprehensions</1> and generator expressions (like Python)
concise function expressions ({1|function(args) expr}; this experimental syntax predated arrow functions)
<1>ECMAScript for XML</1> (E4X), an extension that adds native XML support to ECMAScript (unsupported in Firefox since version 21<2><3><4><5>[</5>86<6>]</6></4></3></2>)
JavaScript syntax
Main article: <1>JavaScript syntax</1>
Variable (computer science)
<1>Variables</1> in JavaScript can be defined using either the {2|var},<3><4><5><6>[</6>87<7>]</7></5></4></3> {8|let}<9><10><11><12>[</12>88<13>]</13></11></10></9> or {14|const}<15><16><17><18>[</18>89<19>]</19></17></16></15> keywords. Variables defined without keywords will be defined at the global scope.
Arrow functions were first introduced in <1>6th Edition – ECMAScript 2015</1>. They shorten the syntax for writing functions in JavaScript. Arrow functions are <2>anonymous</2>, so a variable is needed to refer to them in order to invoke them after their creation, unless surrounded by parenthesis and executed immediately.

Comment (computer programming)
Slash (punctuation)
Note the <1>comments</1> in the examples above, all of which were preceded with two <2>forward slashes</2>.
